=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a positive integer N and two strings S and T, each of length N and consisting of lowercase English letters.
Determine whether it is possible to make S identical to T by repeating the operation below any number of times (possibly zero). If it is possible, also find the minimum number of operations required.

- Choose two lowercase English letters x, y and replace every occurrence of x in S with y.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
S
T

Output

If it is possible to make S identical to T, print the minimum number of operations required. Otherwise, print -1.

Constraints


- 1\leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- N is an integer.
- Each of S and T is a string of length N, consisting of lowercase English letters.

Sample Input 1

6
afbfda
bkckbb

Sample Output 1

4

By performing the operation four times in the following way, you can make S identical to T:

- Choose x= b and y= c. S becomes afcfda.
- Choose x= a and y= b. S becomes bfcfdb.
- Choose x= f and y= k. S becomes bkckdb.
- Choose x= d and y= b. S becomes bkckbb, which is identical to T.

It cannot be done with fewer than four operations, so the minimum number of operations required is 4.

Sample Input 2

4
abac
abac

Sample Output 2

0

S and T are already identical, so no operations are required.

Sample Input 3

4
abac
abrc

Sample Output 3

-1

No matter how you repeat the operation, it is impossible to make S identical to T.

Sample Input 4

4
abac
bcba

Sample Output 4

4

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's consider operation sequence as composing maps. We need find minimal number of point maps g_i to achieve h extending p. This is equivalent to: We have 26 "registers" (letters) each initially containing a distinct token? Actually positions of same letter are indistinguishable tokens. Operation x->y moves all tokens currently in register x to register y. We need for each initial letter a in A, its tokens end in register p(a). We can choose final positions for tokens of letters not in A arbitrarily. Min moves.

This is like "pebble motion" on complete graph with 26 vertices, where each vertex can hold any number of tokens, and a move transfers all tokens from one vertex to another. We need move sets of tokens. Since tokens of same initial letter must stay together? Operation moves all tokens in a vertex, so tokens from different initial letters can merge and then cannot split. Thus final sets are partitions of initial letters. The mapping p induces a partition: letters with same p(a) must be merged (their tokens end in same register). Letters with different p(a) must not be merged at any point? If they merge, they cannot split, so they must have same final target. Thus p must be consistent with merging; if p(a)!=p(b), their tokens must never be in same register at any time. This imposes constraints.

A move x->y merges the contents of x into y. After that, x is empty. So an empty register is needed to avoid merging incompatible groups. We can also merge compatible groups intentionally.

We need transform initial tokens (one group per letter in A) to final groups: for each target letter t, all a with p(a)=t should be in register t. If t not in A, register t initially empty and final contains group.

This is exactly "sorting colored balls with one operation: move all balls of a color to another color". There is known minimum = number of colors to move + number of cycles - (something).

Let's consider the directed graph of p. Each vertex a has an edge to p(a). If p(a)=a, no move needed. If p(a)=b, we need move group a to b. But if b also needs to move to c, group a cannot be moved to b before b moves to c, else it would move again. So order along edges.

For a tree/forest acyclic, process edges in reverse topological order: for each edge u->v, move u to v after v has been emptied (i.e., after v's group moved to p(v)). This uses exactly one move per non-self edge. No extra empty needed? Let's check if no empty and graph acyclic with self-loops, e.g., a->a, b->a. Process a? a self-loop no move, b->a moves b to a. Works. If chain b->a, a->c, c->c? Actually a->c, b->a, c->c. Need final b=a,a=c,c=c. Process c self-loop no move; a->c moves a to c; b->a moves b to a. Works. No empty needed because moving a to c frees a before moving b to a. This is reverse topological. So acyclic graphs require ans moves, no empty.

For cycles, reverse topological impossible because every vertex in cycle needs to move to next, but no vertex to free first. Need an empty register. If an empty register exists, use it to break cycle with one extra move per cycle. If no empty register, need create one by merging a non-cycle group into another compatible group. This is possible if there is a non-cycle vertex. If all vertices are in cycles (permutation), no merge possible without violating final distinctness, impossible.

This matches formula.

Now, what if there are multiple cycles and no empty but a non-cycle vertex. We can create one empty by merging that non-cycle vertex into some group. But will that merging be compatible? Need choose a non-cycle vertex u. Since u not in a cycle, p(u) != u. We need move u's group to some register v such that final target of u equals final target of v's contents? Actually if we move u to v, u's tokens join v. They will share final register. For final correctness, u and all tokens in v at that moment must have the same final target. Initially v contains tokens of original letter v (unless v already moved). If v has not been moved yet, its final target is p(v). We need p(u) = p(v) to merge u with v safely. If p(u) != p(v), merging would be bad. But in example c->a, p(c)=a, we moved c to b where p(b)=a, so p(c)=p(b). Good. In example d->a, p(d)=a, moved d to b where p(b)=a. So choose v such that p(v)=p(u). Is there always such v for some non-cycle u? If u is a leaf? Let's see.

Given a non-cycle vertex u, p(u)=w. If w has a predecessor v in the graph (some vertex v with p(v)=w), then p(v)=w=p(u), so moving u to v is safe. Does w always have a predecessor other than u? In a functional graph, every vertex that is in a cycle has a predecessor in the cycle. If w is not in a cycle but has indegree from u only, then no other v with p(v)=w. But if w is not in a cycle, then w is also a non-cycle vertex. Maybe choose u = w? We need find some non-cycle vertex whose target has another predecessor. In a finite tree feeding into a cycle, the vertex closest to the cycle (parent of cycle? Actually edge from tree node to cycle vertex) has target in cycle, which has a cycle predecessor. So we can choose u as a tree node whose parent is in a cycle. Such a node exists if there is any non-cycle vertex? In a functional graph, every tree component has at least one edge into a cycle (or into missing vertex if empty). If no empty and there is non-cycle vertex, there is at least one tree node u with p(u) in a cycle. Then p(u) is a cycle vertex t. Let v be the predecessor of t in the cycle (p(v)=t). Then p(v)=t=p(u), so moving u to v is safe. Good. If the only non-cycle vertices are in a tree feeding into a missing vertex? But no empty means no missing vertex. So yes.

If empty exists, we don't need merge.

Thus formula stands.

But wait, what if no empty and there is a non-cycle vertex, but all non-cycle vertices are in a tree that feeds into a self-loop, not a length≥2 cycle? Then cycles c=0, answer ans, no need create empty. If c>0, there is at least one length≥2 cycle. In each component with a length≥2 cycle, if there is any tree node feeding into that cycle, we can use it. But what if the only non-cycle vertices are in components with self-loops, and the length≥2 cycles have no tree nodes? Then p would be permutation? Let's examine. Suppose alphabet all 26 appear. There is a 2-cycle a<->b with no tree nodes attached (indegree exactly 1 for a,b from each other). There is also a self-loop c->c with a tree d->c. Yes, d is empty after moving d->c (or c processed). Then use d as temp for a<->b. No need tree attached to cycle. So any non-cycle vertex anywhere can create empty. Good.

What if no empty and p has a length≥2 cycle, and also a self-loop component with no tree? That means self-loop vertex c has indegree only from itself, no tree. Then every vertex is in a cycle (2-cycle + self-loops) => p is permutation, impossible. If there is any tree anywhere, possible.

So condition: if no empty and p is a permutation on all 26 letters (i.e., every letter appears and p is bijective), then non-self cycles impossible. Actually in a functional graph on 26 vertices with outdegree 1, if every vertex is in a cycle, then number of cycles (including self-loops) covers all vertices. We can detect if there is a non-cycle vertex by checking if any vertex has indegree 0? In a functional graph, every vertex in a cycle has indegree at least 1 from cycle. If a vertex has indegree 0, it's not in a cycle. But if no indegree 0, could still have trees? Example a->b, b->a, c->b. Indegrees: a from b, b from a,c, c 0. So indegree 0 exists. If every vertex indegree >=1 in finite graph with outdegree 1, then it's a permutation (each component cycle with trees? Actually if every vertex indegree >=1 and outdegree=1, then total indegree=26=outdegree, so indegree exactly 1 for all, so permutation, all cycles). Thus if no empty and p defined for all, we can check if every vertex has indegree >=1. If yes, p is permutation. If not, there is a non-cycle vertex. Method 1: DFS with state.
state = [0]*26  # 0 unvisited, 1 in current path, 2 done
cycle_count = 0
for i in range(26):
    if p[i] != -1 and p[i] != i and state[i] == 0:
        cur = i
        path = []
        while cur != -1 and state[cur] == 0 and p[cur] != -1 and p[cur] != cur:
            state[cur] = 1
            path.append(cur)
            cur = p[cur]
        # After loop:
        # cur could be -1 (edge to letter not in S? But p[cur]? Wait cur is current vertex; if p[cur] == -1, then cur is a vertex with no outgoing. We didn't mark cur? Actually we mark cur before moving. Let's trace.
        # If cur is a vertex with p[cur] == -1, the while condition p[cur] != -1 fails before marking cur? We need mark cur? Let's design better.
        if cur != -1 and state[cur] == 1:
            cycle_count += 1
        for node in path:
            state[node] = 2
But need handle if cur is a vertex with p[cur]==-1 or p[cur]==cur. In while condition, we check state[cur]==0 and p[cur]!=-1 and p[cur]!=cur.         # But what if cur is a vertex already state=1 from current path, cycle found. Need ensure we didn't skip marking cur due to p[cur]==cur? If p[cur]==cur, state[cur] would be 0 (we never mark self-loops), so no cycle. Good.
        # What if cur is state=2, no cycle.

- For u in 0..25:

1 c->d: a b d d (orig c becomes d)
2 a->c: c b d d
3 b->a: c a d d
4 c->b: b a d d = T. 4. Good.

Now, what if no empty and p a<->b, c->d, d->a (tree into cycle) K=4 all appear. Edges a,b,c,d =4, cycles=1 -> answer5. We did similar. Good.

Now, let's consider a more complex graph with a cycle and a tree that branches? Functional graph outdegree 1, indegree can be multiple. Trees can branch into same node. We only need one non-cycle vertex to create empty. If there is a non-cycle vertex, possible. If no empty and all on_cycle, impossible. Good.

Now, let's think about "minimum number of operations" if p has cycles and has_empty True but ans+cycles maybe overcounts if a cycle can be resolved "for free" by an operation that is already needed for a tree edge? In example c->a into cycle, ans+cycles=4, and we used c->b twice, a->c, b->a. The extra operation beyond ans is one extra c->b. Could the extra be avoided by using the tree edge c->a as part of cycle? Suppose operations: c->a, a->b, b->a? Let's see p a<->b,c->a. Try c->a first: original c becomes a, a occupied orig a+c. Then a->b: all a (orig a+c) become b. Then b->a: all b (orig a+c+orig b) become a. Final all a. Not. Try a->b, b->a, c->a: a->b: orig a b, b b, c c. b->a: orig a a, b a, c c. c->a: all a. Not. So need extra. Lower bound ans+c likely.

Let's try to prove lower bound for cycles with tree maybe: Each operation can reduce the number of "unfinished cycles" by at most one? Not needed.

But let's ensure formula ans+cycles not ans+cycles-1 when there is a non-cycle vertex. We tested small K=3 with p a<->b,c->a and found 4. Let's brute force all sequences length 3 for K=3 to be absolutely sure? We reasoned but let's do a quick exhaustive mental or systematic.

K=3, initial groups A,B,C. Target: A->B, B->A, C->A. We need final partition: {B,C} in A? Actually final register A contains B and C, register B contains A. So groups B and C merge into A. Operation count 3 means exactly 3 moves. Since groups A,B,C all need to move? Group A moves to B, group B moves to A, group C moves to A. That's 3 groups, so each group must be moved exactly once (no group can be moved twice because that would require >3 moves or some group not moved). But in our 4-op sequence, group C was moved twice? Original C moved c->b, then b->a: actually original C moved twice (c->b then b->a). Group A moved a->c then c->b: also twice. Group B moved b->a: once. Total moves 4, some groups moved twice. With 3 moves, each group exactly once. Is there a sequence where each group exactly once and final correct? Let's enumerate possible one-move-per-group. The operation for A must be A->?; for B B->?; for C C->?. Since final A=B, B=A, C=A. If A moves to B, then later B move will affect A unless B move occurs before A move. If B moves before A, B->A, then A move A->B affects B (original B at A) too, making B final B not A. If order C? Let's consider all 3! orders of moving A,B,C once.

If order A first: A->B. Then B later B->? affects original A. For final A=B, if B->A, original A becomes A wrong; if B->C, original A C wrong. So A cannot be first unless no later B move, but B must move. If B first: B->A. Then A later affects B. For final B=A, if A->B, original B becomes B wrong; if A->C, B C wrong. So B cannot be first. If C first: C->A. Then A and B later affect C. Final C=A, but A later A->? will affect C (since C at A) unless A move before C? Contradiction. Thus no 3-op sequence. So 4 min. Good.

Now, what if no empty and p has a cycle and a non-cycle vertex, but the non-cycle vertex's target is not "ready" because it's in a tree that ultimately feeds into the same cycle? We handled with moving to predecessor. Need ensure there is always at least one operation we can do to create empty without disturbing final. Suppose graph component is a cycle with a tree attached. Let u be a leaf? If we move a leaf u to its parent p(u), u becomes empty. Is it safe? p(u) may not be final yet. If p(u) is not a cycle vertex but another tree node, moving u to p(u) before p(u) moves could cause u to move again when p(u) moves, but that's okay if final of u equals final of p(u)? In example c->d->e self-loop, we didn't move c->d early; we moved d->e to create empty. So choose a non-cycle vertex whose target is a cycle vertex or self-loop? More generally, to create empty safely with one operation, choose a vertex u such that p(u) is a "sink" or cycle vertex whose final value equals p(u)? Let's analyze.

We need perform an operation x->y that makes x empty and is safe (doesn't ruin final). If x's original tokens move to y. For final, they must end at p(x). If later y changes, they will change. So we need either:
- y is not changed later, and y = p(x) (so final p(x)); or
- y is changed later to p(x) (i.e., final h(y)=p(x)).
If y is a cycle vertex, final h(y)=p(y) (next in cycle). So need p(y)=p(x). Thus choose y with p(y)=p(x). If y is a self-loop, p(y)=y, need p(x)=y, so y=p(x), and y not changed later. If y is a tree node, final h(y)=p(y), need p(y)=p(x), but y itself will move later, so x tokens would move with y to p(y), final p(x) if p(y)=p(x). Actually if y moves later to p(y), x tokens at y move to p(y). If p(y)=p(x), final correct. So condition p(y)=p(x) is sufficient regardless of y being tree/cycle, as long as y's final value is p(y) and y's move occurs after x->y? Wait if y moves later, x tokens at y move to p(y). If p(y)=p(x), final correct. So moving x to any y with p(y)=p(x) is safe, provided y's own final is p(y). But if y is a tree node and p(y) maybe not final? p(y) is by definition final target of y. If y moves to p(y), x tokens end p(y)=p(x). Good. So condition p(y)=p(x) enough.

Thus to create empty, find a non-cycle vertex x and another vertex y with p(y)=p(x). If x's parent p(x) has a predecessor y (some vertex mapping to p(x)), then safe. In a functional graph, every vertex t that is in the image of p has at least one predecessor. If p(x)=t, then x itself is a predecessor of t. Need another predecessor y != x? If x is the only predecessor of t, then no y with p(y)=t except x. But if x is the only predecessor of t, then t has indegree 1 from x. If t is not in a cycle? Let's see. In a functional graph, if t has indegree 1 and t is not in a cycle, then following from t eventually reaches a cycle. There will be some vertex on that path with indegree >=2? Not necessarily. Example chain x->t->t (self-loop). t has indegree from x and self-loop? Self-loop p(t)=t contributes indegree to t, so indegree of t is 2 (from x and itself). So t has predecessor y=t with p(t)=t=p(x). Good. Example chain x->t->u->u (self-loop). t has indegree from x only (u has indegree from t and self-loop). p(x)=t. Is there y != x with p(y)=t? No. Can we move x to t safely? p(t)=u, not t, so x would become u, wrong. Can we create empty from t instead? t has p(t)=u, and u has predecessor? u self-loop p(u)=u, so p(u)=u, p(t)=u, so moving t to u is safe (t->u) and t becomes empty. So choose x=t, not leaf x. In general, along a tree path to a cycle/self-loop, choose the vertex just before a vertex that has another predecessor? Actually the vertex whose parent has another predecessor. In a tree feeding into a cycle, the node whose parent is in the cycle has parent t in cycle, and t has cycle predecessor y with p(y)=t. So safe. In a tree feeding into a self-loop, the node whose parent is the self-loop has parent t with p(t)=t, so y=t works. In a chain x->t->u->u, choose t (parent u self-loop) because p(u)=u=p(t), move t->u safe. So there is always a non-cycle vertex x such that p(x) is in a cycle (including self-loop) or has another predecessor. Specifically, take any non-cycle vertex that is closest to a cycle (i.e., its parent is in a cycle). Then parent t is in a cycle, so t has a predecessor y in that cycle (if t in length>=2 cycle, y is previous cycle vertex; if t self-loop, y=t). p(y)=t=p(x). Moving x->y is safe and makes x empty. This operation is not necessarily p(x), but counts as one operation. Then use x as temp. Good. So construction possible.

But our formula ans+cycles counts total operations; the operation x->y is one operation. The required edge x->p(x) may not be performed; instead x's final achieved through y's final. Total operations count: We use one operation to move x to y (creating temp). Then for each cycle, we use cycle length +1 operations? Let's count. For a cycle of length L, using temp x, we need L+1 operations: move first cycle vertex to temp, then process cycle, then move temp to target. In example a<->b with temp c: a->c, b->a, c->b = 3 = L+1. For multiple cycles, same temp reused, each cycle costs L+1. The tree operation x->y is not part of cycle cost? In example c->a into cycle, x=c, y=b (predecessor of a), operation c->b creates temp. Then cycle a<->b costs a->c, b->a, c->b =3. Total 4. ans = edges: a->b,b->a,c->a =3. cycles=1. ans+cycles=4. Here the tree operation c->b is not the required edge c->a, but it replaces it. The cycle cost includes an extra operation c->b at the end (temp to target) which is same operation label c->b as the first? Actually operation c->b appears twice; total operations: c->b (create temp), a->c, b->a, c->b (finish). The required edge c->a is not used. So ans counted c->a, but we used c->b twice. Total 4 = ans (a,b,c edges) + 1. So the tree operation is effectively used both to create temp and to finish cycle? It's counted as one of ans? Let's map: ans includes c->a, but operation c->b is not c->a. So the "ans" lower bound by source letters is still 3 (a,b,c each selected at least once). We selected c twice, a once, b once. Total 4. So extra operation is the second c->b. The first c->b is the required operation for source c (though target different). So ans counts source c's first operation. Thus total ans+cycles.

If there are multiple cycles, the temp creation operation for source x is counted in ans. Each cycle adds one extra operation beyond the L operations for its cycle vertices? Wait cycle vertices operations are counted in ans (each cycle vertex source). For a cycle of length L, ans includes L operations for those vertices. Using temp, we perform L+1 operations involving cycle vertices? In 2-cycle: operations a->c (a source), b->a (b source), c->b (extra, not a source in cycle). So L+1, extra 1. For 3-cycle: a->temp, c->a, b->c, temp->b. Operations: a (source), c (source), b (source), temp (extra). L+1. So each cycle adds one extra operation (temp->target). The temp creation operation x->y is counted in ans for source x. So total ans + number_of_cycles. Good.

If there is an empty temp, no need creation operation; each cycle adds one extra. ans+cycles.

If no empty but non-cycle vertex, creation operation is one of ans (for that non-cycle source). So ans+cycles.

If no empty and all vertices in cycles, no non-cycle source to use as temp and no empty; impossible for non-identity permutation.

Thus formula solid.

Now, let's test a tricky case: N=26, S contains all letters, T is a permutation with a 2-cycle and rest identity. Our algorithm:

Now, let's ensure cycle_count counts each cycle once. With 26 letters, simple.

Let's write code robustly.

Pseudo:

import sys

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    p = [-1]*26
    present = [False]*26
    for a,b in zip(S,T):
        u = ord(a)-97; v = ord(b)-97
        present[u] = True
        if p[u] == -1:
            p[u] = v
        elif p[u] != v:
            print(-1)
            return
    ans = 0
    for u in range(26):
        if p[u] != -1 and p[u] != u:
            ans += 1
    if ans == 0:
        print(0)
        return

    # count cycles length >=2
    state = [0]*26
    cycles = 0
    for i in range(26):
        if p[i] != -1 and p[i] != i and state[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []
            while cur != -1 and state[cur] == 0 and p[cur] != -1 and p[cur] != cur:
                state[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                cur = p[cur]
            if cur != -1 and state[cur] == 1:
                cycles += 1
            for node in path:
                state[node] = 2
    if cycles == 0:
        print(ans)
        return

    has_empty = not all(present)
    if has_empty:
        print(ans + cycles)
        return

    # no empty: all 26 letters appear in S, so p[u] != -1 for all u
    # determine if every vertex is on a directed cycle (including self-loop)
    indeg = [0]*26
    for u in range(26):
        v = p[u]
        if v != -1:
            indeg[v] += 1
    from collections import deque
    q = deque([i for i in range(26) if indeg[i] == 0])
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        v = p[u]
        if v != -1:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                q.append(v)
    on_cycle = [indeg[i] > 0 for i in range(26)]
    if all(on_cycle):
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(ans + cycles)

But wait: In no empty case, p[u] != -1 for all u because all letters appear in S. We set p for every u. So indeg sum 26. If all(on_cycle) true, p is permutation. If cycles>0, impossible. Good.

That's correct for original x (final t) and original y (final t). Good. Then later t moves to next, but original x should final t, not next. Wait! Hold on. In a cycle, p(y)=t, so original y's final is t. But t's final is next, not t. If we move y->t, original y tokens go to t. Later, if t is moved to next, original y would move to next, wrong! Ah! I made a serious mistake. Let's revisit.

In a cycle y -> t -> ... -> y, p(y)=t, p(t)=next. Final of original y should be t, not next. If we perform operation y->t before t->next, then original y tokens at t will be affected by t->next and become next, wrong. Therefore, to set y's final to t, we must move y->t after t has been moved to next (so color t is empty and won't be changed again). In the standard cycle rotation with temp, the order is not simply y->t then t->next; it's reverse: first move some vertex to temp, then process from end to start. For cycle y -> t -> ... -> y, standard sequence with temp x:
v1? Let's define cycle order v1 -> v2 -> ... -> vk -> v1.
To preserve each vi final vi+1, we need process vk->v1 first? Wait earlier for 3-cycle a->b,b->c,c->a, sequence was a->temp, c->a, b->c, temp->b. That is:
- Move a (v1) to temp.
- Move c (v3) to a (v1). This sets c final a. Since a has been emptied (original a moved to temp), c->a is safe.
- Move b (v2) to c (v3). Since c has been emptied (original c moved to a), b->c safe.
- Move temp (original a) to b (v2). Since b emptied, safe.
So order is: v1->temp, vk->v1, v_{k-1}->vk, ..., v2->v3, temp->v2. This is reverse along cycle starting from v1.

Now, if we have original x tokens at y (v1) because we moved x->y before cycle. Then in the cycle sequence, first step v1->temp moves original y and original x to temp. But original y final is v2, correct. Original x final is v2, correct. Then v2 later? In the sequence, v2 is moved to v3 before temp->v2? Let's list for cycle v1->v2->v3->v1 (3-cycle):
v1->temp
v3->v1
v2->v3
temp->v2
So v2 is moved to v3 before temp->v2. Thus after temp->v2, v2 will not be moved again. So original y and x at v2 final. Good. So safe. My earlier concern was about doing y->t before t->next; standard sequence avoids that by moving v1 to temp first, then processing backwards. If original x tokens are at v1, they get moved to temp, not directly to t. So safe.

Thus construction with x->y (x to predecessor y) before cycle is safe because original x tokens at y will be moved to temp in first cycle step, then to p(y) at end. Good.

Now, let's consider if p has a cycle and has_empty False, all(on_cycle) False, but the

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys
from collections import deque

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    S = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()

    p = [-1] * 26
    present = [False] * 26

    for a, b in zip(S, T):
        u = ord(a) - 97
        v = ord(b) - 97
        present[u] = True
        if p[u] == -1:
            p[u] = v
        elif p[u] != v:
            print(-1)
            return

    ans = 0
    for u in range(26):
        if p[u] != -1 and p[u] != u:
            ans += 1

    if ans == 0:
        print(0)
        return

    # Count directed cycles of length >= 2
    state = [0] * 26
    cycles = 0

    for i in range(26):
        if p[i] != -1 and p[i] != i and state[i] == 0:
            cur = i
            path = []
            while cur != -1 and state[cur] == 0 and p[cur] != -1 and p[cur] != cur:
                state[cur] = 1
                path.append(cur)
                cur = p[cur]

            if cur != -1 and state[cur] == 1:
                cycles += 1

            for v in path:
                state[v] = 2

    if cycles == 0:
        print(ans)
        return

    # If there is at least one letter not appearing in S, it can be used as a buffer.
    has_empty = not all(present)
    if has_empty:
        print(ans + cycles)
        return

    # No empty letter.  If every letter is on a cycle, p is a permutation,
    # and a non-identity permutation cannot be realized without an empty letter.
    indeg = [0] * 26
    for u in range(26):
        v = p[u]
        if v != -1:
            indeg[v] += 1

    q = deque([i for i in range(26) if indeg[i] == 0])
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        v = p[u]
        if v != -1:
            indeg[v] -= 1
            if indeg[v] == 0:
                q.append(v)

    on_cycle = [indeg[i] > 0 for i in range(26)]

    if all(on_cycle):
        print(-1)
    else:
        print(ans + cycles)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
